Let me create a comprehensive analysis document: ## Thorough Analysis of `_sync_triage_module_bindings` Pattern --- ### Overview The `_sync_triage_module_bindings()` function in `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage_handlers.py` is a **monkeypatch-friendly rebinding mechanism** that dynamically propagates imported functions into split triage submodules. It exists to enable **test monkeypatching at a single point** while ensuring those patches are visible throughout the modular triage subsystem. --- ### What It Does Located in `triage_handlers.py` lines 33-76, the function manually assigns module-level references: ```python def _sync_triage_module_bindings() -> None: """Propagate monkeypatch-friendly bindings into split triage modules.""" _helpers_mod.command_runtime = command_runtime _helpers_mod.save_plan = save_plan _display_mod.command_runtime = command_runtime _display_mod.collect_triage_input = collect_triage_input _display_mod.detect_recurring_patterns = detect_recurring_patterns _display_mod.load_plan = load_plan _confirmations_mod.append_log_entry = append_log_entry _confirmations_mod.collect_triage_input = collect_triage_input _confirmations_mod.command_runtime = command_runtime # ... etc for all 6 triage submodules ``` **It is called at two entry points:** 1. Line 80: `_cmd_triage_start()` — before executing the start subcommand 2. Line 127: `cmd_plan_triage()` — at the top of the main triage command dispatcher **Modules being synced (6 total):** - `_helpers_mod` (helpers.py) - `_display_mod` (display.py) - `_confirmations_mod` (confirmations.py) - `_stages_mod` (stages.py) - `_flow_mod` (_stage_flow_commands.py) - `_completion_mod` (_stage_completion_commands.py) - `_validation_mod` (_stage_validation.py) **Functions being bound:** - `command_runtime` - `save_plan` - `load_plan` - `collect_triage_input` - `detect_recurring_patterns` - `append_log_entry` - `extract_issue_citations` --- ### The Problem It Solves: Test Monkeypatching The reason this pattern exists is **to enable tests to mock these functions at a single point**. **Evidence from tests** (`test_triage_attestation.py`, lines 206-244): ```python def test_confirm_observe_rejects_generic_attestation(self, monkeypatch, capsys): """Attestation of 80+ chars that doesn't mention any dimension is rejected.""" plan = _plan_with_stages("observe") state = _state_with_issues("r1", "r2", "r3", dimension="naming") # Monkeypatch at the HANDLER module level, not the submodule monkeypatch.setattr(triage_mod, "load_plan", lambda *a, **kw: plan) monkeypatch.setattr(triage_mod, "command_runtime", lambda args: _fake_runtime(state)) monkeypatch.setattr(triage_mod, "require_completed_scan", lambda s: True) # Call the command... args = _fake_args(confirm="observe", attestation=attestation) triage_mod.cmd_plan_triage(args) # ← This calls _sync_triage_module_bindings() # Now confirmations.py, display.py, etc. see the mocked functions out = capsys.readouterr().out ``` **Why the sync is necessary:** 1. Each submodule **imports these functions directly at the top** (e.g., `from desloppify.engine.plan import load_plan`) 2. When the test monkeypatches `triage_mod.load_plan`, it only affects the `triage_handlers` module 3. The submodules still reference the **original imported binding** from `desloppify.engine.plan` 4. The sync function **updates the module-level namespace** of each submodule: `_helpers_mod.load_plan = load_plan` (where `load_plan` is now the monkeypatched version) 5. When submodule code executes, Python looks up `load_plan` in its own module namespace and finds the **synced reference** --- ### Architecture Diagram ``` […]

File Contents Summary **22 language-specific import resolver functions:** | Language | Function | LOC | |----------|----------|-----| | Go | `resolve_go_import` | 32 | | Rust | `resolve_rust_import` | 34 | | Ruby | `resolve_ruby_import` | 22 | | Java | `resolve_java_import` | 26 | | Kotlin | `resolve_kotlin_import` | 20 | | C/C++ | `resolve_cxx_include` | 23 | | PHP | `resolve_php_import` | 20 | | Elixir | `resolve_elixir_import` | 26 | | Lua | `resolve_lua_import` | 18 | | Swift | `resolve_swift_import` | 30 | | Scala | `resolve_scala_import` | 17 | | C# | `resolve_csharp_import` | 24 | | Dart | `resolve_dart_import` | 26 | | JavaScript | `resolve_js_import` | 18 | | Bash | `resolve_bash_source` | 29 | | Perl | `resolve_perl_import` | 25 | | Zig | `resolve_zig_import` | 28 | | Haskell | `resolve_haskell_import` | 26 | | Erlang | `resolve_erlang_include` | 28 | | OCaml | `resolve_ocaml_import` | 26 | | F# | `resolve_fsharp_import` | 32 | | R | `resolve_r_import` | 30 | **Utility/Constants:** - `_camel_to_snake()` — 5 LOC (used only by Elixir resolver) - `_PERL_SKIP_MODULES` — frozenset with ~8 core modules - `_PERL_SKIP_PREFIXES` — tuple with ~6 prefixes - `_HASKELL_STDLIB_PREFIXES` — tuple with ~8 stdlib prefixes - `_OCAML_STDLIB_MODULES` — frozenset with ~20 stdlib modules - `_FSHARP_STDLIB_PREFIXES` — tuple with 3 prefixes **Module overhead:** ~93 LOC (docstring, imports, `__all__` list) ### 2. Resolver Architecture Patterns The resolvers fall into **two clear families** (though with overlap): **Family A: Relative-Path Resolvers** (resolve relative imports + search source dirs) - Ruby, C++, Bash, Erlang, Zig, Dart, R, Swift - Typical: start with file directory, add .extension, try fallback roots - LOC range: 18–30 **Family B: Module-Path Resolvers** (namespace to filesystem mapping) - Java, Kotlin, Scala, C#, PHP, Haskell, OCaml, F#, Lua, Elixir, JS - Typical: split namespace by delimiter (`.`, `::`), map to directory structure - Special cases: Go (module path + file), Rust (crate:: + multiple patterns) - LOC range: 17–34 **Shared Patterns Across All:** 1. **"Search multiple source roots"** — Java, Kotlin, Scala, C#, Haskell, OCaml, F#, Ruby, Perl, R 2. **"Dot-to-path conversion"** — Java, Kotlin, Scala, Lua, Elixir, PHP, Haskell, F# 3. **"Strip quotes/extension handling"** — Bash, Erlang, Zig, R, Perl, C++ 4. **"Filter stdlib/core modules"** — Perl, Haskell, OCaml, F# 5. **"Try multiple file extensions"** — […]

The review pipeline completed successfully. Here is a full summary of what happened: --- ## Authorization Consistency Review Pipeline - Results ### Pipeline Execution **Step 1 — Prepare:** Succeeded. Identified 578 files in codebase, organized into 2 investigation batches: - Batch 1: Authorization (1 file) — targeted `authorization_consistency` - Batch 2: Design Coherence - Mechanical Concern Signals (80 files) — mapped to other dimensions **Step 2 — Run Batches:** Initial run with default runner was blocked (open backlog: 593 objective issues). The fallback `--runner codex --force-review-rerun` command was used. Batch 1 succeeded, Batch 2 failed because the `codex` CLI is not installed on PATH. Batch 1 was then re-run using `--only-batches 1 --force-review-rerun` and successfully imported. ### Authorization Consistency Findings (4 issues discovered by batch 1) The reviewer found 4 issues in the `authorization_consistency` dimension, but only **3 net new issues** were imported (1 was resolved): Issues found: 1. **`auth_guard_heuristics_drift`** — Auth guard detection logic diverges between `review/context_signals/auth.py` (which uses `requireAuth`, `withAuth`, `getServerSession` patterns) and `typescript/detectors/security.py` (which only accepts `authenticateRequest`, `auth.getUser`, etc). This causes inconsistent classification of the same code across the two subsystems. Fix: create a shared auth-guard pattern contract in a single module. 2. **`global_middleware_auth_missed`** — `gather_auth_context()` only marks routes as protected when `_AUTH_GUARD_RE` appears inside a route segment. File-level/router-level middleware guards (e.g., `app.use(requireAuth)`) applied before route declarations are invisible to coverage analysis. Fix: augment route coverage logic to detect middleware-level protections. 3. **`rls_view_check_unreachable_in_ts_phase`** — `_check_rls_bypass()` in `typescript/detectors/security.py` only triggers on `.sql` files, but the TypeScript scan phase only receives `.ts/.tsx` files. The SQL RLS view bypass check is effectively dead code in normal TypeScript scans. Fix: move SQL RLS checks to a cross-language detector or extend phase input to include SQL files. 4. **`schema_qualified_rls_collision`** — `_SCHEMA_QUALIFIED_IDENT` regex discards the schema prefix and only keys RLS coverage by bare table name. This means `public.users` and `audit.users` collapse to the same key, causing incorrect coverage reporting for same-named tables across schemas. Fix: use fully-qualified `schema.table` keys throughout. ### Score After Import **Authorization Consistency: 64.8** (assessment updated via trusted internal durable import) Full dimension assessments after the import: | Dimension | Score | |---|---| | authorization_consistency | **64.8** | | low_level_elegance | 68.0 | | naming_quality | 65.0 | | ai_generated_debt | 65.5 | | logic_clarity | 62.6 | | type_safety | 61.7 | | incomplete_migration | 54.5 | | test_strategy | 54.3 | | contract_coherence | 51.0 | | […]

Multiple detector/extractor files (3-4 each) ### Assessment: **MOSTLY FALSE POSITIVES FOR THIS CONTEXT** **Reasoning:** 1. **Subprocess calls (44 of 56 issues):** These are almost entirely **legitimate and necessary** for a CLI tool. The tool is designed to invoke external code analysis tools (git, ruff, pyflakes, bandit, jscpd, knip, and others). All subprocess calls follow safe patterns: - Hard-coded command names (not user input): `["git", "rev-parse", "HEAD"]` - Explicit tool name with array format, avoiding shell=True (except 1 case) - Timeouts specified (e.g., 5-60 seconds) - Exception handling for FileNotFoundError and TimeoutExpired - Commands run on the local machine against the project being scanned **Example from git_context.py** (11 flagged, all safe): ```python subprocess.run(["git", "rev-parse", "HEAD"], capture_output=True, timeout=_GIT_TIMEOUT) ``` This is a **read-only observation** of git state — appropriate for a local CLI tool. 2. **MD5 hash for "security" (7 issues):** These are **false positives**. MD5 is used to compute `body_hash` in function extractors for **duplicate detection and change tracking**, NOT for cryptographic security: ```python body_hash = hashlib.md5(normalized.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest() ``` MD5 is perfectly acceptable for this use case (fast, stable, collision-unlikely for code fingerprinting). Bandit's B324 rule is overly broad for non-security contexts. 3. **URL open audit (1 issue):** In `update_skill.py` (line 30), the code safely downloads a skill document from the desloppify GitHub repository: ```python with urllib.request.urlopen(url, timeout=15) as resp: # noqa: S310 ``` The URL is hard-coded to the project's own GitHub repo, and there's already a `noqa` comment indicating this was reviewed. **Safe and appropriate.** 4. **Weak RNG (1 issue):** In `_fix_preview.py`, one use of pseudo-random generation flagged. Here is my complete assessment. --- ## Honest Assessment of 5 Signature Drift Issues ### Issue 1: `import_holistic_issues` -- 3 different signatures across 3 files **Actual signatures found:** | File | Signature | |------|-----------| | `desloppify/intelligence/review/importing/holistic.py:262` | `def import_holistic_issues(issues_data: ReviewImportPayload, state: StateModel, lang_name: str, *, project_root: Path \| str \| None = None, utc_now_fn=utc_now) -> dict[str, Any]` | | `desloppify/intelligence/review/__init__.py:66` | `def import_holistic_issues(*args: Any, **kwargs: Any) -> dict[str, Any]` | | `desloppify/tests/review/context/test_holistic_review.py:111` | `def import_holistic_issues(issues_data, state, lang_name, **kwargs)` | **Verdict: Noise.** The `__init__.py` version is a *lazy import wrapper* -- its entire purpose is to accept any args and forward them to the real implementation to break an import cycle. It literally does `return _import_holistic_issues(*args, **kwargs)`. The test version is a *convenience helper* that wraps the real function to normalize the payload format (converting bare lists to `{"issues": ...}` dicts). Neither is an independent implementation that could drift. The production caller (`cmd.py:398`) passes `(issues_data, working_state, lang.name)` which matches both the real signature and the wrapper. No bug risk. --- ### Issue 2: `import_review_issues` -- 3 different signatures across 3 files **Actual signatures found:** | File | Signature | |------|-----------| | `desloppify/intelligence/review/importing/per_file.py:58` | `def import_review_issues(issues_data: ReviewImportPayload, state: StateModel, lang_name: str, *, project_root: Path \| str \| None = None, utc_now_fn=utc_now) -> dict[str, Any]` | | `desloppify/intelligence/review/__init__.py:57` | `def import_review_issues(*args: Any, **kwargs: Any) -> dict[str, Any]` | | `desloppify/tests/review/review_coverage_cases.py:71` | `def import_review_issues(issues_data, state, lang_name)` | **Verdict: Noise.** Exact same pattern as Issue 1. The `__init__.py` version is the same lazy import wrapper. The test version is a thin convenience wrapper that normalizes the input payload before calling the real `_import_review_issues_impl`. The breadth of `PLAN_LOAD_EXCEPTIONS` (ImportError, AttributeError, OSError, ValueError, TypeError, KeyError) means a genuine bug in any of those `_sync_*` helper functions could be silently swallowed. However, plan reconciliation is explicitly a post-scan best-effort operation -- if it fails, the scan results are still saved. The risk is that a programming error in one of those helpers goes undetected for a while. This is the most legitimately concerning item in the cluster, but it is not "genuinely poor" -- it is a deliberate defensive strategy for a non-critical subsystem. --- ### 4. `preflight.py` line 45 -- PLAN_LOAD_EXCEPTIONS during score cleanup **Code:** ```python try: plan = load_plan() […]

**`TestResolveParallelRuntime`** (8 tests) -- worker count capping, custom workers, default fallback on invalid input, heartbeat enable/disable/zero/negative. - **`TestCompleteParallelFuture`** (6 tests) -- uses real `concurrent.futures.Future` objects (pre-resolved): success code 0 not in failures, nonzero code added to failures, exception handled via error_log_fn, prior progress failure taints success, missing started_at falls back to clock_fn, and progress callback receives the correct done event. - Design a refactor to make the subjective dimension visibility gating in desloppify beautifully engineered. The current code has the decision "should this subjective item appear in the work queue?" scattered across multiple layers. The user wants it properly engineered — clean, simple, one clear responsibility per component. ## Current Architecture (messy) The decision is spread across: 1. **`build_subjective_items`** in `synthetic.py` (lines 223-318) — generates candidates. Sets metadata fields `initial_review`, `stale_review`, `open_review_issues`. The `stale_review` and `open_review_issues` fields are **dead code** (never read by any consumer). Also computes `review_open_by_dim` which is used for the `primary_command` field (whether to show review issues or suggest a new review). 2. **`_gather_subjective_items`** in `core.py` (lines 176-228) — has an `open_objective_count > 0` early return that blocks ALL subjective items while there's mechanical work. Then applies SubjectiveVisibility policy per-item. Then applies plan_queue_set override. 3. **`SubjectiveVisibility`** dataclass in `subjective_policy.py` — has a `should_surface()` method that checks `initial_review` flag and `has_objective_backlog`. But the early return in `_gather_subjective_items` means `should_surface()` is only ever called when `open_objective_count == 0`, at which point `has_objective_backlog` is always False, so `should_surface()` always returns True. **The policy is effectively dead for queue filtering.** 4. **`sync_stale_dimensions`** in `stale_dimensions.py` — injects/evicts subjective IDs from `plan.queue_order`. This is plan mutation (separate concern from queue filtering). It uses SubjectiveVisibility for its own injection decisions. 5. **`compute_subjective_visibility`** — creates the policy snapshot. Used by both queue filtering and plan sync. Has a more nuanced objective count (excludes suppressed, evidence-only, out-of-scope). The `_gather_subjective_items` early return uses a simpler count (just `detector != "review"`). ## Key Facts - `initial_review` field: actively consumed by ranking.py (sort priority), subjective_policy.py (should_surface), and plan injection - `stale_review` field: **dead code** — set but never read by any consumer - `open_review_issues` field: **dead code** — set in detail dict but never rendered or used - `SubjectiveVisibility.should_surface()`: effectively dead for queue filtering (always True when called), but the dataclass IS used by `sync_stale_dimensions` for plan mutation decisions - Plan `queue_order` override: the code has a `plan_queue_set` mechanism but the early return prevents it from ever overriding for subjective items ## Design Goals 1. **One place decides** if subjective items appear in the queue. Not three. 2. **Dead code removed** — `stale_review`, `open_review_issues` fields gone 3. **Each function has one job** — `build_subjective_items` builds candidates, `_gather_subjective_items` gates them 4. **The objective count check should be consistent** — use the same logic everywhere (currently two different counting methods) 5. **`SubjectiveVisibility`** […]

Here is the complete analysis and refactoring plan. --- ## Diagnosis: Three Overlapping Decision Points The current code makes the same decision -- "should subjective items appear in the work queue?" -- in three different places, with three different counting methods, and only one of them actually matters at runtime. ### Decision Point 1: `_gather_subjective_items` early return (core.py, lines 212-218) ```python open_objective_count = sum( 1 for iss in issues.values() if iss.get("status") == "open" and iss.get("detector") != "review" ) if open_objective_count > 0: return [] ``` This is a blunt count: any open non-review issue blocks all subjective items. It does not exclude suppressed issues, evidence-only issues, out-of-scope issues, or plan-skipped issues. It also counts `concerns`, `subjective_review`, and `subjective_assessment` detectors as "non-review" even though they are non-objective. This is the **only gate that actually runs** for queue filtering. It is overly broad -- it counts things that `compute_subjective_visibility` intentionally excludes. ### Decision Point 2: `SubjectiveVisibility.should_surface()` (subjective_policy.py, lines 34-41) ```python def should_surface(self, item: dict) -> bool: if item.get("initial_review"): return True return not self.has_objective_backlog ``` This has a more nuanced objective count (via `compute_subjective_visibility`): excludes suppressed, evidence-only, out-of-scope, plan-skipped, and all `NON_OBJECTIVE_DETECTORS`. It also has per-item logic for `initial_review`. But it is **effectively dead for queue filtering** -- it only runs after the early return passes (meaning `open_objective_count == 0`), at which point `has_objective_backlog` is always False (because the more nuanced count is a subset of the simpler count), so `should_surface()` always returns True. ### Decision Point 3: `plan_queue_set` override (core.py, line 225) ```python if not policy.should_surface(item) and item_id not in plan_queue_set: continue ``` This intends to let the plan override the policy and force subjective items to appear. But the early return on line 217 fires before this code ever runs, so the plan override for subjective items is also dead code. ### Dead metadata fields - **`stale_review`** (synthetic.py line 315): Set on every subjective item but never read by any consumer. Not consumed by ranking, rendering, or any other module. - **`detail["open_review_issues"]`** (synthetic.py line 308): Set in the detail dict but never read by any downstream consumer. The `open_review` count itself is still needed because it determines `primary_command` (line 290-293). Only its storage in the detail dict is dead. Two tests (`work_queue_cases.py` lines 237, 279) assert on this dead field. ### The counting inconsistency The early […]
